========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, thank you. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I actually need to pay attention to at work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of my day is reports and meetings and clinic metrics, and I’ll sit down to start an EPIC quality report with every intention of focusing on it. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.

Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.

And in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, R41.0, F32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I actually need to pay attention to at work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of my day is reports and meetings and clinic metrics, and I’ll sit down to start an EPIC quality report with every intention of focusing on it. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.\n\nOr I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.

I mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.

I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating when you're doing things you enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.\n\nI mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.

With hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.

I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nPatient: I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.\n\nWith hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.

It’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.

I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.\n\nOr I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating when you're doing things you enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.\n\nI mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nPatient: I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.\n\nWith hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.

But whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.

I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.

But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.

Sometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.

I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.

But if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.

So… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.

I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.

That’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.

And outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.

Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating when you're doing things you enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.\n\nI mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nPatient: I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.\n\nWith hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "R41.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. If I think about activities I enjoy, I don’t feel like, “Oh, I don’t care about that anymore,” or like they’ve lost their appeal across the board.

Sometimes I may not get around to doing them as much, or I’ll feel like the effort of organizing it or starting it is a bit much, but that feels different from not being interested. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I actually need to pay attention to at work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of my day is reports and meetings and clinic metrics, and I’ll sit down to start an EPIC quality report with every intention of focusing on it. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.\n\nOr I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating when you're doing things you enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.\n\nI mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nPatient: I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.\n\nWith hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. that I was bright but careless, and my mother had to sit with me while I finished homework because I would lose track of assignments or forget books at school.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines and making other teams wait on me, and I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since my promotion about two years and four months ago, when the larger portfolio started overwhelming the systems I used to rely on.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. If I think about activities I enjoy, I don’t feel like, “Oh, I don’t care about that anymore,” or like they’ve lost their appeal across the board.\n\nSometimes I may not get around to doing them as much, or I’ll feel like the effort of organizing it or starting it is a bit much, but that feels different from not being interested. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so—not in a major way, at least. I mean, I can be impulsive in small, ordinary ways, like saying yes to something before I’ve fully thought through how much time it will take, or maybe buying something minor without comparing options forever. But I don’t usually do things where I look back and think, “That was really reckless,” or where there are serious consequences because I acted without thinking.

If anything, I probably overthink a lot of decisions. I may delay deciding because I’m trying to be sure I’m not missing something. So I wouldn’t say impulsive behavior is one of the main issues for me. I can make quick choices sometimes, but I’m generally pretty aware of consequences, especially if it affects work or other people.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I actually need to pay attention to at work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of my day is reports and meetings and clinic metrics, and I’ll sit down to start an EPIC quality report with every intention of focusing on it. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.\n\nOr I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating when you're doing things you enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.\n\nI mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nPatient: I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.\n\nWith hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. If I think about activities I enjoy, I don’t feel like, “Oh, I don’t care about that anymore,” or like they’ve lost their appeal across the board.\n\nSometimes I may not get around to doing them as much, or I’ll feel like the effort of organizing it or starting it is a bit much, but that feels different from not being interested. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a major way, at least. I mean, I can be impulsive in small, ordinary ways, like saying yes to something before I’ve fully thought through how much time it will take, or maybe buying something minor without comparing options forever. But I don’t usually do things where I look back and think, “That was really reckless,” or where there are serious consequences because I acted without thinking.\n\nIf anything, I probably overthink a lot of decisions. I may delay deciding because I’m trying to be sure I’m not missing something. So I wouldn’t say impulsive behavior is one of the main issues for me. I can make quick choices sometimes, but I’m generally pretty aware of consequences, especially if it affects work or other people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think feeling overwhelmed can make it worse, definitely, but it doesn’t feel like the whole explanation. What finally pushed me to come in is that even when I’m not panicking or upset, I can sit down with every intention of focusing—like starting an EPIC quality report or being in a clinic metrics meeting—and within a few minutes I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.

It’s embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the numbers, or listening to someone talk through the metrics, and then suddenly I’m rereading the same line or the same set of figures because none of it actually stuck. Or in a meeting I’ll kind of “come back” and realize I missed the point of what was just discussed, even though I was physically there and trying to pay attention.

So yes, anxiety or being overwhelmed probably adds to it, especially when I know something is important. But the focus problem feels like it happens even when I’m trying hard and even when I care about the task. That’s the part that worries me.

Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them.
