    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks other than work or reports, such as reading a book or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, but honestly the place I notice it most—and the reason I finally came in—is with the work-type things where I really can’t afford to drift off. Then I have to go back and reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because it’s like my eyes were there but my brain wasn’t.\n\nAnd it happens in clinic metrics meetings too. I’ll be sitting there trying to track what people are saying, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I missed the whole point of the discussion. That’s what’s so frustrating, because I’m not choosing to tune out, and I’m not thinking, “Oh, this doesn’t matter.” I know it matters. I’m trying to pay attention.\n\nWith something like a book or a movie, I guess there are times I lose the thread too, but the work examples stand out more because the consequences feel bigger and it’s more obvious when I have to reread numbers or I realize I missed what someone just explained.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus in certain settings, like at home, compared to others, like at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s as simple as home versus work, honestly. I notice it most at work, but I think that’s partly because the tasks make it really obvious when my attention has slipped.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can sit down with an EPIC quality report, completely intending to track it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted so much that I’m rereading the same numbers and still not really absorbing them. Sometimes I’m just… gone mentally, even though I’m looking right at the report.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then I sort of snap back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just said. That’s embarrassing and frustrating, because these are things I know are important. So I wouldn’t say it only happens in one setting, but work is where I feel it most because it affects things I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often forget things that you were supposed to do or remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, that definitely happens. It’s not usually that I forget the whole situation existed, exactly—it’s more like I remember part of it and then don’t carry it all the way through.\n\nLike after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up steps. In my head I’ll think, okay, I’ve got that, I know what needs to happen. And I may do the first thing pretty quickly, and maybe I’ll even start drafting the second thing, like an email or putting something into a spreadsheet. But then somehow the last piece just sits there unfinished. The email stays in drafts, or the spreadsheet is open but not completed, or the final submission doesn’t actually get sent.\n\nAnd then someone reminds me, and I feel awful because it’s not that I didn’t care or didn’t understand what needed to happen. I just… didn’t finish the chain. It’s embarrassing, especially when it’s something that came directly from a meeting and I should have been able to follow through on it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant difficulties with memory, such as forgetting recently learned information or important dates?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it as significant memory trouble in that sense. Like, I don’t think I’m forgetting important dates in a way that feels unusual, and I’m not walking away from something I just learned and having it completely disappear from my mind.

It’s more that I can lose track of what I was supposed to do with the information, if that makes sense. But for dates or facts themselves—birthdays, appointments, major things—I usually have systems for those, like calendars and reminders, so I don’t know if I’d notice it as much. I wouldn’t want to say I’m having major memory problems, because that doesn’t quite feel accurate.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.\n\nOr I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a major way, at least. I mean, I can be impulsive in small, ordinary ways, like saying yes to something before I’ve fully thought through how much time it will take, or maybe buying something minor without comparing options forever. But I don’t usually do things where I look back and think, “That was really reckless,” or where there are serious consequences because I acted without thinking.\n\nIf anything, I probably overthink a lot of decisions. I may delay deciding because I’m trying to be sure I’m not missing something. So I wouldn’t say impulsive behavior is one of the main issues for me. I can make quick choices sometimes, but I’m generally pretty aware of consequences, especially if it affects work or other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think feeling overwhelmed can make it worse, definitely, but it doesn’t feel like the whole explanation. What finally pushed me to come in is that even when I’m not panicking or upset, I can sit down with every intention of focusing—like starting an EPIC quality report or being in a clinic metrics meeting—and within a few minutes I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the numbers, or listening to someone talk through the metrics, and then suddenly I’m rereading the same line or the same set of figures because none of it actually stuck. Or in a meeting I’ll kind of “come back” and realize I missed the point of what was just discussed, even though I was physically there and trying to pay attention.\n\nSo yes, anxiety or being overwhelmed probably adds to it, especially when I know something is important. But the focus problem feels like it happens even when I’m trying hard and even when I care about the task. That’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it equally hard to focus when you're in quieter, less demanding environments compared to busy or noisy ones?\n\nPatient: Not really equally, no. A busy or noisy environment definitely makes it harder, because there’s more to pull my attention away. But what bothers me is that it still happens even when things are quieter and I should be able to concentrate.\n\nLike, I can sit down with an EPIC quality report in a quiet office, fully intending to go through it carefully, and a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same numbers without actually absorbing them. I’ll reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because I got to the end and didn’t retain what I just looked at.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. Even if the room isn’t chaotic, I’ll be trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the main point of what was just said. So noise and busyness make it worse, but the focus problem isn’t only because of that. It shows up even in settings where, on paper, I should be able to track things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: It can be, but I have a hard time pinning it only to a time of day. I’ve tried to notice that, because I keep wondering if there’s some obvious pattern I’m missing, like maybe mornings are better or afternoons are worse. But honestly, the thing that pushed me to come in is that it can happen even when I sit down intending to focus, regardless of whether it’s a “good” time or not.\n\nFor example, I can start an EPIC quality report and think, okay, I’m going to go through this carefully, I need to understand these numbers, and then within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted. I’ll be looking right at the same set of numbers and realize I didn’t actually take them in, so I have to reread the same section. Sometimes it feels like I’ve technically been “working” on it, but I haven’t really absorbed anything.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings, it’s similar. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just being said. That’s the part that bothers me most — not that I’m tired at the end of the day or something predictable like that, but that even when I’m trying to track something important, my attention can just slip away within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks other than work or reports, such as reading a book or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, but honestly the place I notice it most—and the reason I finally came in—is with the work-type things where I really can’t afford to drift off. Like I’ll start an EPIC quality report with the full intention of getting through it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the same numbers but not actually taking them in. Then I have to go back and reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because it’s like my eyes were there but my brain wasn’t.\n\nAnd it happens in clinic metrics meetings too. I’ll be sitting there trying to track what people are saying, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I missed the whole point of the discussion. That’s what’s so frustrating, because I’m not choosing to tune out, and I’m not thinking, “Oh, this doesn’t matter.” I know it matters. I’m trying to pay attention.\n\nWith something like a book or a movie, I guess there are times I lose the thread too, but the work examples stand out more because the consequences feel bigger and it’s more obvious when I have to reread numbers or I realize I missed what someone just explained.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus in certain settings, like at home, compared to others, like at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s as simple as home versus work, honestly. I notice it most at work, but I think that’s partly because the tasks make it really obvious when my attention has slipped.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can sit down with an EPIC quality report, completely intending to track it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted so much that I’m rereading the same numbers and still not really absorbing them. It’s not like I’m distracted by something dramatic every time. Sometimes I’m just… gone mentally, even though I’m looking right at the report.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then I sort of snap back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just said. That’s embarrassing and frustrating, because these are things I know are important. And I may do the first thing pretty quickly, and maybe I’ll even start drafting the second thing, like an email or putting something into a spreadsheet. But then somehow the last piece just sits there unfinished. Like, I don’t think I’m forgetting important dates in a way that feels unusual, and I’m not walking away from something I just learned and having it completely disappear from my mind.\n\nIt’s more that I can lose track of what I was supposed to do with the information, if that makes sense. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. If I think about activities I enjoy, I don’t feel like, “Oh, I don’t care about that anymore,” or like they’ve lost their appeal across the board.\n\nSometimes I may not get around to doing them as much, or I’ll feel like the effort of organizing it or starting it is a bit much, but that feels different from not being interested. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a major way, at least. I mean, I can be impulsive in small, ordinary ways, like saying yes to something before I’ve fully thought through how much time it will take, or maybe buying something minor without comparing options forever. But I don’t usually do things where I look back and think, “That was really reckless,” or where there are serious consequences because I acted without thinking.\n\nIf anything, I probably overthink a lot of decisions. I may delay deciding because I’m trying to be sure I’m not missing something. So I wouldn’t say impulsive behavior is one of the main issues for me. I can make quick choices sometimes, but I’m generally pretty aware of consequences, especially if it affects work or other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think feeling overwhelmed can make it worse, definitely, but it doesn’t feel like the whole explanation. What finally pushed me to come in is that even when I’m not panicking or upset, I can sit down with every intention of focusing—like starting an EPIC quality report or being in a clinic metrics meeting—and within a few minutes I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the numbers, or listening to someone talk through the metrics, and then suddenly I’m rereading the same line or the same set of figures because none of it actually stuck. Or in a meeting I’ll kind of “come back” and realize I missed the point of what was just discussed, even though I was physically there and trying to pay attention.\n\nSo yes, anxiety or being overwhelmed probably adds to it, especially when I know something is important. But the focus problem feels like it happens even when I’m trying hard and even when I care about the task. That’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it equally hard to focus when you're in quieter, less demanding environments compared to busy or noisy ones?\n\nPatient: Not really equally, no. A busy or noisy environment definitely makes it harder, because there’s more to pull my attention away. But what bothers me is that it still happens even when things are quieter and I should be able to concentrate.\n\nLike, I can sit down with an EPIC quality report in a quiet office, fully intending to go through it carefully, and a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same numbers without actually absorbing them. I’ll reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because I got to the end and didn’t retain what I just looked at.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. Even if the room isn’t chaotic, I’ll be trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the main point of what was just said. So noise and busyness make it worse, but the focus problem isn’t only because of that. It shows up even in settings where, on paper, I should be able to track things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: It can be, but I have a hard time pinning it only to a time of day. I’ve tried to notice that, because I keep wondering if there’s some obvious pattern I’m missing, like maybe mornings are better or afternoons are worse. But honestly, the thing that pushed me to come in is that it can happen even when I sit down intending to focus, regardless of whether it’s a “good” time or not.\n\nFor example, I can start an EPIC quality report and think, okay, I’m going to go through this carefully, I need to understand these numbers, and then within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted. I’ll be looking right at the same set of numbers and realize I didn’t actually take them in, so I have to reread the same section. Sometimes it feels like I’ve technically been “working” on it, but I haven’t really absorbed anything.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings, it’s similar. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just being said. That’s the part that bothers me most — not that I’m tired at the end of the day or something predictable like that, but that even when I’m trying to track something important, my attention can just slip away within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks other than work or reports, such as reading a book or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, but honestly the place I notice it most—and the reason I finally came in—is with the work-type things where I really can’t afford to drift off. Like I’ll start an EPIC quality report with the full intention of getting through it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the same numbers but not actually taking them in. Then I have to go back and reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because it’s like my eyes were there but my brain wasn’t.\n\nAnd it happens in clinic metrics meetings too. I’ll be sitting there trying to track what people are saying, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I missed the whole point of the discussion. That’s what’s so frustrating, because I’m not choosing to tune out, and I’m not thinking, “Oh, this doesn’t matter.” I know it matters. I’m trying to pay attention.\n\nWith something like a book or a movie, I guess there are times I lose the thread too, but the work examples stand out more because the consequences feel bigger and it’s more obvious when I have to reread numbers or I realize I missed what someone just explained.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus in certain settings, like at home, compared to others, like at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s as simple as home versus work, honestly. I notice it most at work, but I think that’s partly because the tasks make it really obvious when my attention has slipped.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can sit down with an EPIC quality report, completely intending to track it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted so much that I’m rereading the same numbers and still not really absorbing them. It’s not like I’m distracted by something dramatic every time. Sometimes I’m just… gone mentally, even though I’m looking right at the report.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then I sort of snap back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just said. That’s embarrassing and frustrating, because these are things I know are important. So I wouldn’t say it only happens in one setting, but work is where I feel it most because it affects things I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often forget things that you were supposed to do or remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, that definitely happens. It’s not usually that I forget the whole situation existed, exactly—it’s more like I remember part of it and then don’t carry it all the way through.\n\nLike after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up steps. In my head I’ll think, okay, I’ve got that, I know what needs to happen. And I may do the first thing pretty quickly, and maybe I’ll even start drafting the second thing, like an email or putting something into a spreadsheet. But then somehow the last piece just sits there unfinished. The email stays in drafts, or the spreadsheet is open but not completed, or the final submission doesn’t actually get sent.\n\nAnd then someone reminds me, and I feel awful because it’s not that I didn’t care or didn’t understand what needed to happen. I just… didn’t finish the chain. It’s embarrassing, especially when it’s something that came directly from a meeting and I should have been able to follow through on it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with memory, such as forgetting recently learned information or important dates?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as significant memory trouble in that sense. Like, I don’t think I’m forgetting important dates in a way that feels unusual, and I’m not walking away from something I just learned and having it completely disappear from my mind.\n\nIt’s more that I can lose track of what I was supposed to do with the information, if that makes sense. But for dates or facts themselves—birthdays, appointments, major things—I usually have systems for those, like calendars and reminders, so I don’t know if I’d notice it as much. I wouldn’t want to say I’m having major memory problems, because that doesn’t quite feel accurate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it goes back a long way, honestly. I don’t think this is something that suddenly started in the last few years, even though work has made it more obvious and more consequential.

When I think back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright but careless. That phrase came up more than once, or at least that’s how I remember it. Like I understood the material, but I’d miss parts of the assignment or make mistakes that looked like I hadn’t checked my work. And my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t do it, but because I would lose track of what I was supposed to be doing or forget that I had an assignment at all.

I also remember forgetting books at school and then getting home and realizing I didn’t have what I needed to do the homework. So… yes, when I look back, the pattern feels familiar. It’s just that as a kid it got framed as being careless or needing to try harder, and now as an adult there’s a lot more responsibility attached to it.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I actually need to pay attention to at work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of my day is reports and meetings and clinic metrics, and I’ll sit down to start an EPIC quality report with every intention of focusing on it. I know it matters, I know what I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same numbers without really taking them in.\n\nOr I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to follow the discussion, and my mind just… goes somewhere else. Not because I don’t care. I do care. But then suddenly I’m aware that people have moved on and I’ve missed the whole point of what was just said. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m experienced and I should be able to track these conversations. I find myself rereading things over and over or trying to piece together what I missed, and it’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with focusing and paying attention something that you're experiencing only at work, or are you also having trouble concentrating in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s most obvious at work, I think because that’s where the demands are so constant and concrete. Like, I can sit down to do an EPIC quality report and I genuinely mean to focus on it — I’m not avoiding it, I’m not thinking it’s unimportant — but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same section or the same numbers and I’m not actually absorbing them. Then I have to go back and reread, sometimes more than once, which makes everything take longer than it should.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings it’s the same kind of thing. I’ll be trying to follow along, and then my mind just sort of drifts without me noticing it happening. Then suddenly I come back and realize the conversation has moved on and I missed the main point, or I’m trying to infer what was decided based on the last few comments. So work is definitely where I’m noticing it most, because it’s affecting things I really need to track carefully.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating when you're doing things you enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I notice it much more at work because there’s such a clear consequence if I miss something, so it stands out to me there. Outside of work, with things I enjoy, I guess I haven’t really tracked it the same way.\n\nI mean, if I’m doing something pleasant, I’m less likely to think, “Oh, this is a concentration problem,” because there isn’t someone waiting for a report or a meeting moving on without me. So I don’t want to say yes or no too confidently. I just know the work situations are where it’s been obvious enough that I felt like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing when you're doing leisure activities or hobbies that you normally find engaging?\n\nPatient: I guess the honest answer is that I notice it much more when the activity has some kind of demand attached to it. Like at work, it’s very clear to me: I can sit down with an EPIC quality report fully intending to follow the data, and within a few minutes I realize I’m just rereading the same numbers and not really taking them in. Or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, trying to stay with the discussion, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I’ve missed the whole point of what people were talking about.\n\nWith hobbies or leisure things, it’s harder for me to say as clearly, because there isn’t the same consequence if my mind drifts. I may just put something down or lose the thread and not label it as a problem. But the pattern I’m most aware of is that even when I’m interested or I know something matters, my attention can slip away before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, I’ve definitely felt frustrated and worn down by what’s been going on, especially when I can’t get through things the way I expect myself to. But “sad” or “empty” or depressed… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I get discouraged in the moment, because I feel like I should be able to just sit down and do what needs to be done, and then I’m annoyed with myself when it takes so much effort. But I haven’t really been walking around feeling sad all the time, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can probably think of moments where I’ve jumped in because I thought I knew where someone was going, or I was worried I’d lose the thought if I didn’t say it right then, but I don’t know that I’d say I’m constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nAt work especially, I try pretty hard to be measured, because there are a lot of meetings and discussions where it would be really noticeable if I talked over people. So I’m aware of trying not to do that. I may occasionally cut in by accident, but I don’t think that’s the main issue I’ve noticed. It’s not something people have really pointed out to me, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely disorganized or that I can’t manage my time at all. I do have systems, and in my job I sort of have to keep track of a lot of moving pieces, so I’ve built up ways to manage it.\n\nBut whether it feels effective… that’s where I hesitate. Sometimes it takes a lot more effort than I think it should. I’ll make lists, put things on my calendar, set reminders, and then I still end up spending too much time figuring out what to start with, or I underestimate how long something is going to take. And then the day gets away from me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by trouble organizing tasks, though. It’s not like my life is chaos from the outside, necessarily. It’s more that internally it can feel like I’m constantly trying to keep all the pieces from scattering, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: With friends or family… I don’t think it’s often, no. I mean, I probably do it occasionally, especially if the conversation is casual and moving quickly, but I haven’t noticed it as a major pattern. My family hasn’t really said, “You’re always interrupting,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll jump in because I think of something and I want to say it before I forget, but I’m also pretty conscious of not wanting to be rude. So if anything, I might start to say something and then stop myself, or I’ll say, “Sorry, go ahead.” But blurting out answers before someone finishes… not really in a way that stands out to me. It’s possible I do it more than I realize, but it’s not something I’d identify as one of the main issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I feel physically restless all the time, like I need to get up and walk around constantly. I can sit through meetings or a dinner or something like that without it being obvious, I think.\n\nBut if I’m sitting still for a long time, I may shift around, cross and uncross my legs, play with a pen, adjust in my chair—things like that. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until I catch myself or realize I’ve been moving my foot for a while. It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. If I think about activities I enjoy, I don’t feel like, “Oh, I don’t care about that anymore,” or like they’ve lost their appeal across the board.\n\nSometimes I may not get around to doing them as much, or I’ll feel like the effort of organizing it or starting it is a bit much, but that feels different from not being interested. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a major way, at least. I mean, I can be impulsive in small, ordinary ways, like saying yes to something before I’ve fully thought through how much time it will take, or maybe buying something minor without comparing options forever. But I don’t usually do things where I look back and think, “That was really reckless,” or where there are serious consequences because I acted without thinking.\n\nIf anything, I probably overthink a lot of decisions. I may delay deciding because I’m trying to be sure I’m not missing something. So I wouldn’t say impulsive behavior is one of the main issues for me. I can make quick choices sometimes, but I’m generally pretty aware of consequences, especially if it affects work or other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think feeling overwhelmed can make it worse, definitely, but it doesn’t feel like the whole explanation. What finally pushed me to come in is that even when I’m not panicking or upset, I can sit down with every intention of focusing—like starting an EPIC quality report or being in a clinic metrics meeting—and within a few minutes I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the numbers, or listening to someone talk through the metrics, and then suddenly I’m rereading the same line or the same set of figures because none of it actually stuck. Or in a meeting I’ll kind of “come back” and realize I missed the point of what was just discussed, even though I was physically there and trying to pay attention.\n\nSo yes, anxiety or being overwhelmed probably adds to it, especially when I know something is important. But the focus problem feels like it happens even when I’m trying hard and even when I care about the task. That’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it equally hard to focus when you're in quieter, less demanding environments compared to busy or noisy ones?\n\nPatient: Not really equally, no. A busy or noisy environment definitely makes it harder, because there’s more to pull my attention away. But what bothers me is that it still happens even when things are quieter and I should be able to concentrate.\n\nLike, I can sit down with an EPIC quality report in a quiet office, fully intending to go through it carefully, and a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same numbers without actually absorbing them. I’ll reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because I got to the end and didn’t retain what I just looked at.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. Even if the room isn’t chaotic, I’ll be trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the main point of what was just said. So noise and busyness make it worse, but the focus problem isn’t only because of that. It shows up even in settings where, on paper, I should be able to track things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: It can be, but I have a hard time pinning it only to a time of day. I’ve tried to notice that, because I keep wondering if there’s some obvious pattern I’m missing, like maybe mornings are better or afternoons are worse. But honestly, the thing that pushed me to come in is that it can happen even when I sit down intending to focus, regardless of whether it’s a “good” time or not.\n\nFor example, I can start an EPIC quality report and think, okay, I’m going to go through this carefully, I need to understand these numbers, and then within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted. I’ll be looking right at the same set of numbers and realize I didn’t actually take them in, so I have to reread the same section. Sometimes it feels like I’ve technically been “working” on it, but I haven’t really absorbed anything.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings, it’s similar. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just being said. That’s the part that bothers me most — not that I’m tired at the end of the day or something predictable like that, but that even when I’m trying to track something important, my attention can just slip away within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks other than work or reports, such as reading a book or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, but honestly the place I notice it most—and the reason I finally came in—is with the work-type things where I really can’t afford to drift off. Like I’ll start an EPIC quality report with the full intention of getting through it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the same numbers but not actually taking them in. Then I have to go back and reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because it’s like my eyes were there but my brain wasn’t.\n\nAnd it happens in clinic metrics meetings too. I’ll be sitting there trying to track what people are saying, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I missed the whole point of the discussion. That’s what’s so frustrating, because I’m not choosing to tune out, and I’m not thinking, “Oh, this doesn’t matter.” I know it matters. I’m trying to pay attention.\n\nWith something like a book or a movie, I guess there are times I lose the thread too, but the work examples stand out more because the consequences feel bigger and it’s more obvious when I have to reread numbers or I realize I missed what someone just explained.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus in certain settings, like at home, compared to others, like at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s as simple as home versus work, honestly. I notice it most at work, but I think that’s partly because the tasks make it really obvious when my attention has slipped.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can sit down with an EPIC quality report, completely intending to track it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted so much that I’m rereading the same numbers and still not really absorbing them. It’s not like I’m distracted by something dramatic every time. Sometimes I’m just… gone mentally, even though I’m looking right at the report.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then I sort of snap back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just said. That’s embarrassing and frustrating, because these are things I know are important. So I wouldn’t say it only happens in one setting, but work is where I feel it most because it affects things I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often forget things that you were supposed to do or remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, that definitely happens. It’s not usually that I forget the whole situation existed, exactly—it’s more like I remember part of it and then don’t carry it all the way through.\n\nLike after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up steps. In my head I’ll think, okay, I’ve got that, I know what needs to happen. And I may do the first thing pretty quickly, and maybe I’ll even start drafting the second thing, like an email or putting something into a spreadsheet. But then somehow the last piece just sits there unfinished. The email stays in drafts, or the spreadsheet is open but not completed, or the final submission doesn’t actually get sent.\n\nAnd then someone reminds me, and I feel awful because it’s not that I didn’t care or didn’t understand what needed to happen. I just… didn’t finish the chain. It’s embarrassing, especially when it’s something that came directly from a meeting and I should have been able to follow through on it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with memory, such as forgetting recently learned information or important dates?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as significant memory trouble in that sense. Like, I don’t think I’m forgetting important dates in a way that feels unusual, and I’m not walking away from something I just learned and having it completely disappear from my mind.\n\nIt’s more that I can lose track of what I was supposed to do with the information, if that makes sense. But for dates or facts themselves—birthdays, appointments, major things—I usually have systems for those, like calendars and reminders, so I don’t know if I’d notice it as much. I wouldn’t want to say I’m having major memory problems, because that doesn’t quite feel accurate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus and attention for as long as you can remember, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back a long way, honestly. I don’t think this is something that suddenly started in the last few years, even though work has made it more obvious and more consequential.\n\nWhen I think back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright but careless. That phrase came up more than once, or at least that’s how I remember it. Like I understood the material, but I’d miss parts of the assignment or make mistakes that looked like I hadn’t checked my work. And my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t do it, but because I would lose track of what I was supposed to be doing or forget that I had an assignment at all.\n\nI also remember forgetting books at school and then getting home and realizing I didn’t have what I needed to do the homework. So… yes, when I look back, the pattern feels familiar. It’s just that as a kid it got framed as being careless or needing to try harder, and now as an adult there’s a lot more responsibility attached to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s not usually this intense feeling like I’m going to jump out of my skin, though.\n\nSo… I guess some fidgeting, yes, but I don’t know that I’d call restlessness one of the bigger issues for me. It’s more subtle, or at least I think it is.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your daily life where you feel like your difficulty with focusing and paying attention is causing significant problems or interference?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the main place it’s become hard to dismiss is work. I’m a medical services manager, so a lot of what I do depends on keeping track of reporting pieces and deadlines and making sure information moves from one group to another. And lately—or maybe not even lately, but it’s become more visible lately—my delays have started affecting clinic reporting deadlines. It’s not just me feeling stressed privately anymore; other teams are sometimes waiting on me to finish something so they can do their part.\n\nThat’s really uncomfortable to admit, because I’m used to being someone who can manage things, and I do care about being reliable. But I’ll get bogged down trying to focus on the report, or I’ll have pieces of it partly done, and then I’m slower than I should be pulling it together. Then suddenly it’s not just “I’m behind,” it’s “now the clinic reporting timeline is getting squeezed” or someone else has to follow up with me.\n\nAnd outside of work, the clearest example is volunteer health workshops I’ve helped with. I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the planning because I just couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. There are schedules, materials, people to coordinate, follow-ups… and I found myself dropping the ball or moving too slowly, even though I wanted to do it. So I’m still involved, but I’ve had to take on less of the leadership role, which feels frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a clear decrease in interest, exactly. I mean… I still like the things I’ve usually liked, at least in theory. If I think about activities I enjoy, I don’t feel like, “Oh, I don’t care about that anymore,” or like they’ve lost their appeal across the board.\n\nSometimes I may not get around to doing them as much, or I’ll feel like the effort of organizing it or starting it is a bit much, but that feels different from not being interested. Once I’m actually doing something I enjoy, I can still enjoy it. So I don’t think I’ve noticed a major drop in interest itself. It’s more that I may not follow through on things as consistently as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in a major way, at least. I mean, I can be impulsive in small, ordinary ways, like saying yes to something before I’ve fully thought through how much time it will take, or maybe buying something minor without comparing options forever. But I don’t usually do things where I look back and think, “That was really reckless,” or where there are serious consequences because I acted without thinking.\n\nIf anything, I probably overthink a lot of decisions. I may delay deciding because I’m trying to be sure I’m not missing something. So I wouldn’t say impulsive behavior is one of the main issues for me. I can make quick choices sometimes, but I’m generally pretty aware of consequences, especially if it affects work or other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is related to feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think feeling overwhelmed can make it worse, definitely, but it doesn’t feel like the whole explanation. What finally pushed me to come in is that even when I’m not panicking or upset, I can sit down with every intention of focusing—like starting an EPIC quality report or being in a clinic metrics meeting—and within a few minutes I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the numbers, or listening to someone talk through the metrics, and then suddenly I’m rereading the same line or the same set of figures because none of it actually stuck. Or in a meeting I’ll kind of “come back” and realize I missed the point of what was just discussed, even though I was physically there and trying to pay attention.\n\nSo yes, anxiety or being overwhelmed probably adds to it, especially when I know something is important. But the focus problem feels like it happens even when I’m trying hard and even when I care about the task. That’s the part that worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it equally hard to focus when you're in quieter, less demanding environments compared to busy or noisy ones?\n\nPatient: Not really equally, no. A busy or noisy environment definitely makes it harder, because there’s more to pull my attention away. But what bothers me is that it still happens even when things are quieter and I should be able to concentrate.\n\nLike, I can sit down with an EPIC quality report in a quiet office, fully intending to go through it carefully, and a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same numbers without actually absorbing them. I’ll reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because I got to the end and didn’t retain what I just looked at.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. Even if the room isn’t chaotic, I’ll be trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the main point of what was just said. So noise and busyness make it worse, but the focus problem isn’t only because of that. It shows up even in settings where, on paper, I should be able to track things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your ability to focus is worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: It can be, but I have a hard time pinning it only to a time of day. I’ve tried to notice that, because I keep wondering if there’s some obvious pattern I’m missing, like maybe mornings are better or afternoons are worse. But honestly, the thing that pushed me to come in is that it can happen even when I sit down intending to focus, regardless of whether it’s a “good” time or not.\n\nFor example, I can start an EPIC quality report and think, okay, I’m going to go through this carefully, I need to understand these numbers, and then within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted. I’ll be looking right at the same set of numbers and realize I didn’t actually take them in, so I have to reread the same section. Sometimes it feels like I’ve technically been “working” on it, but I haven’t really absorbed anything.\n\nAnd in clinic metrics meetings, it’s similar. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then suddenly I kind of come back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just being said. That’s the part that bothers me most — not that I’m tired at the end of the day or something predictable like that, but that even when I’m trying to track something important, my attention can just slip away within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks other than work or reports, such as reading a book or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, but honestly the place I notice it most—and the reason I finally came in—is with the work-type things where I really can’t afford to drift off. Like I’ll start an EPIC quality report with the full intention of getting through it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the same numbers but not actually taking them in. Then I have to go back and reread the same section, sometimes more than once, because it’s like my eyes were there but my brain wasn’t.\n\nAnd it happens in clinic metrics meetings too. I’ll be sitting there trying to track what people are saying, and then suddenly I sort of “come back” and realize I missed the whole point of the discussion. That’s what’s so frustrating, because I’m not choosing to tune out, and I’m not thinking, “Oh, this doesn’t matter.” I know it matters. I’m trying to pay attention.\n\nWith something like a book or a movie, I guess there are times I lose the thread too, but the work examples stand out more because the consequences feel bigger and it’s more obvious when I have to reread numbers or I realize I missed what someone just explained.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus in certain settings, like at home, compared to others, like at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s as simple as home versus work, honestly. I notice it most at work, but I think that’s partly because the tasks make it really obvious when my attention has slipped.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can sit down with an EPIC quality report, completely intending to track it carefully, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has drifted so much that I’m rereading the same numbers and still not really absorbing them. It’s not like I’m distracted by something dramatic every time. Sometimes I’m just… gone mentally, even though I’m looking right at the report.\n\nAnd the same thing happens in clinic metrics meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to follow the discussion, and then I sort of snap back and realize I missed the whole point of what was just said. That’s embarrassing and frustrating, because these are things I know are important. So I wouldn’t say it only happens in one setting, but work is where I feel it most because it affects things I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you often forget things that you were supposed to do or remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, that definitely happens. It’s not usually that I forget the whole situation existed, exactly—it’s more like I remember part of it and then don’t carry it all the way through.\n\nLike after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up steps. In my head I’ll think, okay, I’ve got that, I know what needs to happen. And I may do the first thing pretty quickly, and maybe I’ll even start drafting the second thing, like an email or putting something into a spreadsheet. But then somehow the last piece just sits there unfinished. The email stays in drafts, or the spreadsheet is open but not completed, or the final submission doesn’t actually get sent.\n\nAnd then someone reminds me, and I feel awful because it’s not that I didn’t care or didn’t understand what needed to happen. I just… didn’t finish the chain. It’s embarrassing, especially when it’s something that came directly from a meeting and I should have been able to follow through on it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with memory, such as forgetting recently learned information or important dates?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as significant memory trouble in that sense. Like, I don’t think I’m forgetting important dates in a way that feels unusual, and I’m not walking away from something I just learned and having it completely disappear from my mind.\n\nIt’s more that I can lose track of what I was supposed to do with the information, if that makes sense. But for dates or facts themselves—birthdays, appointments, major things—I usually have systems for those, like calendars and reminders, so I don’t know if I’d notice it as much. I wouldn’t want to say I’m having major memory problems, because that doesn’t quite feel accurate.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus and attention for as long as you can remember, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back a long way, honestly. I don’t think this is something that suddenly started in the last few years, even though work has made it more obvious and more consequential.\n\nWhen I think back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright but careless. That phrase came up more than once, or at least that’s how I remember it. Like I understood the material, but I’d miss parts of the assignment or make mistakes that looked like I hadn’t checked my work. And my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t do it, but because I would lose track of what I was supposed to be doing or forget that I had an assignment at all.\n\nI also remember forgetting books at school and then getting home and realizing I didn’t have what I needed to do the homework. So… yes, when I look back, the pattern feels familiar. It’s just that as a kid it got framed as being careless or needing to try harder, and now as an adult there’s a lot more responsibility attached to it."
